ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-16_U15	Moving forward to agenda item 6: Second reading of the draft law amending the collective agreement and other laws initiated by the Government of the Republic. Siret Kotka, Chairman of the Social Affairs Committee, will come and introduce us. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-16_U16	Good chairman of the sitting! Happy Members of the Riigikogu! The Social Affairs Committee dealt with the draft law amending the collective agreement law and other laws between the first reading and the second reading three times: 25 May, 31 May and 3 June. In these three sessions, we examined their opinions and amendments with stakeholders. I will give you a more detailed overview of the opinions of the stakeholders. The representative of the Estonian Chamber of Commerce and Industry stated that the House supports the purpose of the proposed bill, as the law in force is contrary to the Constitution. The representative of the Confederation of Estonian Trade Unions stated that the Union supports the draft because it is designed by agreement between the three parties, the Estonian Employers' Confederation, the Estonian Trade Union Confederation and the Ministry of Social Affairs. The representative of the Estonian Employers' Confederation, like the representative of the Estonian Trade Union Confederation, confirmed that the principle of extending collective agreements has been very much negotiated and supported. The representative of the Estonian Association of Small and Medium Enterprises noted that the proposed EMEA draft law does not support. The Estonian Medical Association supported the draft by proposing one amendment. According to the Union, the majority of persons do not read the explanatory notes to the drafts, so the provisions of the collective agreement law may be misinterpreted and it is not known that there are two negotiations to be held: negotiations on the conclusion of a separate collective agreement and the extension of a separate collective agreement. According to the explanations provided by the Ministry of Social Affairs, the Commission did not change the wording as the practice in the field of health is in place and the text of the law is legally correct. The central organisation of the trade unions of staff, the TALO, submitted its opinion in writing and in principle supported the draft. Three amendments had been tabled to us. The first was presented by the Group of the Social Democratic Party. It aims to remove the representativity rates set out in the draft, as different sectors are of different sizes and for some the degree of representativeness may be too high, for others too low. The extension of the terms of the collective agreement could therefore remain the subject of a collective agreement between workers and employers. The Social Affairs Committee did not support the proposal. The proposal was supported by 2 and 7 members of the Commission opposed. Amendment 2 was tabled by the Committee on Social Affairs. The proposal consists of two proposals. Similar changes will be made at the same time in the Trade Unions Act and in the Workers' Repository Act due to the wording of the Estonian Trade Union Confederation and the Estonian Chamber of Commerce and Industry's recommendation to bring the text of the draft into line with the draft explanatory memorandum. The Social Affairs Committee's proposal was supported by all nine members of the Commission. The third amendment was tabled in the same way by the Committee on Social Affairs. The amendment extends the payment of temporary unemployment benefit and unemployment benefit if the following conditions are met in the month in which the unemployment period ended: the rate of registered unemployment in the previous calendar month was higher than 8,5% and the period of unemployment benefit ends between 1 September 2021 and 28 February 2022. It is necessary to extend the duration of unemployment benefit and unemployment benefit by 60 calendar days if the labour market situation deteriorates, i.e. the registered unemployment rate in Estonia rises by more than 8.5%. An extension of unemployment benefit requires an amendment to the Employment Services and Benefits Act and the Unemployment Insurance Act. The amendment is temporary, as I said before, and is based on the figures for registered unemployment in 2021. If the unemployment rate is to rise by more than 8,5%, depending on the month, the cost of extending the unemployment benefit, together with the social security contribution, may be between EUR 1 and EUR 1,5 million and the cost of unemployment benefit up to EUR 380000 per month. This decision was adopted by consensus on a proposal from the Social Committee. All nine members of the Commission agreed. We also made the following proposals: to propose that the draft be placed on the plenary agenda for second reading on 9 June 2021, to propose that the second reading be closed and, when the second reading is closed, to propose that the draft be placed on the plenary agenda and that the final vote be taken on 16 June inclusive. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-16_U119	Reuben Kaalep, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-16_U120	Honourable Riigikogu! In such a situation, where we, as representatives of the people, have a political dispute in this Chamber, there are views on both sides and it is difficult to find a compromise on how we should proceed to improve the lives of our people and our country, it is always worth taking a moment to take a look at the past. And ask and see how similar problems and situations were solved by our predecessors, our ancestors, especially on the basis of our already rather long and meaningful tradition of statehood. I came to read Maie Pihlamäe's study from 2006 entitled "Unemployment in the Republic of Estonia in the years 1918 to 1940," which, in my opinion, illustrates exactly the same problems and how they were solved in that period in the Republic of Estonia. I am now quoting some of the paragraphs under the heading "Packets to combat unemployment": "There was no unemployment insurance in Estonia during the period between the two World Wars. However, it cannot be said that this issue has not been on the agenda at all. In order to comply with the ILO's recommendation to the countries to establish an effective unemployment insurance system, the Otto Strandman government undertook in May 1919 to develop and enforce a law on the state insurance of all employees against unemployment and to organise public works to alleviate unemployment. In November 1922, the draft "Law against Old Age, Disability and Unemployment" submitted by the Group of the Socialist Labour Party of the Riigikogu was entangled in the Committee on Labour Protection, who considered it premature to introduce the law and, due to the lack of money, to go beyond Estonia. The introduction of unemployment insurance became topical in the years of the global economic crisis as job opportunities narrowed and unemployment increased. Unemployment insurance was demanded out loud by the unemployed, local trade unions and the Estonian Confederation of Workers' Associations. The next draft unemployment insurance law was developed at the Ministry of Education and Social Affairs. On 27 February 1933, the Committee on Combating Unemployment discussed the draft Act on Unemployment Insurance and decided to reject it on the grounds that Estonia does not have the resources to implement the Act in times of crisis, nor will it be foreseen in the future. The Ministry of Economic Affairs also did not support the introduction of unemployment insurance and considered productive work from a national perspective to be the most appropriate way of combating unemployment. At the same time, both compulsory and voluntary unemployment insurance was well expanded in Europe. In 1919, 4.5 million workers, including 3.7 million in Great Britain, and nearly half a million workers, were compulsorily insured against unemployment worldwide. After World War I, Britain followed the example of Italy (1919), Austria (1920), Russia (1922), Poland (1924), Bulgaria (1925), Germany (1927) and other countries. By 1930, just over 36 million workers were covered by compulsory unemployment insurance in 10 countries around the world. 10 million workers from the Soviet Union have not been taken into account, as on October 9, 1930, all labour exchanges were closed in the Soviet Union and unemployment benefits were stopped due to the total elimination of unemployment. In Germany alone, compulsory state unemployment insurance included 15,6 million workers, in Great Britain 12.4, in Italy 4.5, in Austria 1.2, in Poland 1.0 million, etc. In parallel with compulsory unemployment insurance, the voluntary insurance system developed, which was supported by the state authorities with subsidies. In the run-up to World War I, Belgium, Denmark, France and Norway were the only ones who supported voluntary unemployment funds at national level, and after the war the Netherlands, Finland, Spain, Czechoslovakia and Switzerland were added to them. In these nine countries, almost 3 million workers were covered by voluntary insurance in 1930. In Estonia, the only way to reduce unemployment was to mediate work for the unemployed through work exchanges. The job exchanges were responsible for registering jobseekers as well as job vacancies, keeping current records of labour and job movements and collecting information on the state of the labour market. Registration on the labour market was voluntary. In 1924, when labour demand fell sharply as a result of the economic crisis, the government acknowledged the seriousness of the problem and laid the foundations for a system of assistance to the unemployed. From the 1924 state budget, the Ministry of Labour and Welfare received EUR 25 million in credit for helping the unemployed. The money was mainly used to distribute free lunches, food and clothing and footwear to those in need, as well as to organise emergency work. In this respect, the ILO's 1919 recommendation to organise public works during the unemployment period was followed. Petseri County Government and Valga City Government helped the unemployed with emergency work in the spring of 1924. Emergency work was started in Tallinn on 16 July 1924. Their organisation was the Railway Government and the works were carried out during the construction of the Järve station. At the end of the year, the city governments of Tartu, Narva and Pärnu organised emergency work for the first time. The problem of unemployment is eased by the start of seasonal work in industry and agricultural work in the countryside. As the number of jobseekers was expected to increase again in the autumn, after the end of the seasonal work, on 26 November 1924 the government set up a committee to combat unemployment, consisting of the Minister for Labour, Welfare, Money, Roads and Agriculture. The Commission's task was to develop measures to reduce unemployment. At its first meeting on 9 December 1924, the Commission took the view that unemployment could not be combated by supporting the unemployed materially with food, clothing and footwear. The only effective remedy is emergency work, which gives the unemployed a chance to earn their own living. The Commission for Combating Unemployment was a centre for emergency work until October 1926 when the government entrusted the Ministry of Roads with the organisation, supervision and allocation of emergency work and the corresponding credits.' Unfortunately, in the absence of time, I have to drop this extremely exciting text here. With your permission, I can continue this in a later speech. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-16_U130	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, Good technical personnel! With your permission, I'd get a little ahead of myself. I would like to start by again highlighting the fact that this bill is a kind of tripartite compromise between the Estonian Employers' Confederation, the Estonian Trade Union Confederation and the Ministry of Social Affairs. I would like to draw your attention to the fact that, in the initial phase of the formation of trade unions, such a compromise would have seemed not only impossible but also grotesque, I would say. On the other hand, I would like to stress that Marxism, which for a very long time also gave the trade unions a theoretical ideological basis, has, to date, remained the basis of the paradigm of the conflict, but it is still gradually removed from the trade unions, at least in Europe. True, not really, but not really. Traditional ties have survived. It is often believed that this connection is closer than it really is. Thus, both trade unions and Marxism were required to develop a collective identity of the working class. Otherwise there could not be any trade unions, nor would it have occurred to Marx that proletariat is a hegemonist who moves the development of the world from the front, dragging all the other classes of society to his own. This collective identity was necessary and this collective identity could be developed in the course of industrialisation. Try to think about the situation before industrialisation. Look, it was an aggressive society: people lived in the country, they didn't live together, and they were much more individualistic than the working class. Industrialisation itself began at different times in different countries. But again, its model is actually quite similar. Very often, even historians make the mistake of looking somewhere for transition or take-over features, not to mention that under similar circumstances phenomena occur at the same time or not at the very same time, but they develop similar phenomena that do not mean copying. So industrialisation began at different times in different countries, but the model was similar. As we know, it began in England in the second half of the 18th century. In Russia, industrialisation is handled in such a way that theorists say that it can be so and so. Three minutes, please.
ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-16_U131	Please! Three minutes.
ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-16_U132	Some theorists are convinced that it began at the end of the 19th century as a solo concert in industry, and others claim that in fact we can only talk about socialist industrialisation. What matters to our subject is that while Marx was living in Germany, Germany went through a period of industrialisation, i.e. in the mid-19th century and in the decades following the mid-19th century. Well, now I'm getting to the point. Prior to industrialisation, the industry was dominated by a traditional organisational model, which was a relatively small company and a general ordering system. There was no anonymous market. The market did so by ordering something from the craft master, having some helpers, and as much as they ordered, he produced so much. In the process of industrialisation, the market became anonymous, production went to the factory, and a whole new kind of decision had to be made. These small amounts of capital, which were already sufficient, remained insufficient for the start-up and organisation of a new business. And what happened to the workers? The workers began to work under one roof and one guide, and their number increased sharply. Since factories demanded discipline, such machinework had to always begin and end at a certain time as long as the machine worked, so did the worker. The worker could no longer arrange his own rules, but either the machine or its owner set the rules for all the workers. Discipline became necessary, with which, of course, a large number of workers were not satisfied. They had come from the country. There's something in the countryside where a man is his own master, who decides when he's doing some kind of work. It wasn't like that now. Go on, somebody had to herd those workers, right, so there's discipline. Because when the machine stops, it's a loss. Thus came the institution of the foreman. With the expansion of the number of operational operations, their power would also increase in the future. This is where we see the conflict between classical capitalists and workers. I don't want to spend any more of your time right now, but I'm going to go back to this, too. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-16_U134	Yeah, thanks! First of all, I would like to thank my colleague, Mr White, who gave me the opportunity to continue this discussion on history here. Here, too, it is a very interesting moment to observe the various utopian and globalist movements and the flow of thought throughout history, that it is often such a connecting feature that it is trying to find some kind of connecting or so-called higher idea that frees man from his tribal ties, family ties and ties with his country. This can be seen both in the case of 19th century Marxism and, for example, in today's liberal democracy. Another very clear ideological feature that unites is that it is opposed to any dissent, or one ideology is considered to be the only right one. Unfortunately, I cannot extend this issue further, but I will certainly do so in my subsequent speeches. Thank you.

In fact, Adlas based its communication on Paragraph 6 (2) (7) of the Government's regulation on health and safety requirements in the workplace affected by biological hazards, according to which the health risk of workers must be kept to a minimum and the possibility of vaccination should be ensured for workers who are exposed to biological hazards against which an effective vaccine exists. By examining this regulation, it appears that this regulation lays down health and safety requirements for the protection of workers from biological hazards affecting the working environment, which in fact means that the employer has an obligation to protect workers from biological hazards. But the norm to which Adlas refers actually regulates the employer's obligation to reduce the health risk. The same regulation also states that where the risk cannot be eliminated by such measures, the health risk of workers must be reduced to the lowest possible level by applying the following measures: Among other things, the possibility of vaccination must be guaranteed for workers who are exposed to biological risk factors against which I very much emphasise the effectiveness of the vaccine. But here it is forgotten that this point only gives the employee the opportunity to vaccinate, which means that it cannot be made compulsory. According to the Constitution, a person has free will because, in fact, an employer only wants to protect an employee under the regulation, but cannot be put under pressure. Similarly, it is questionable whether there is an effective vaccine. The other questions go on. But I'm sure I'll be able to talk about it today. ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-16_U200	We are moving forward at a good pace to the seventh item on the agenda, which is the second reading of the draft law amending the Code of Criminal Procedure, initiated by the Committee on Legal Affairs. I am pleased to ask Vilja Toomas, Member of the Legal Affairs Committee for the speech chair of the Riigikogu. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-16_U201	Honourable Chairman of the Riigikogu! Ladies and gentlemen, The draft law amending the Code of Criminal Procedure 392 proposes that § 901 of the Code of Criminal Procedure be amended. The Legal Affairs Committee launched a draft law amending the Code of Criminal Procedure on 12 May. The initiation of the draft act has been pushed by a decision of the Court of Justice of the European Union, including in March, which found that the prosecutor's office cannot be regarded as an independent body for the purpose of granting permissions to request the data referred to in paragraphs 2 and 3 of § 1111 of the Electronic Communications Act, and will be issued by a court. The Government of the Republic issued a favourable opinion on 392 including 24 May. The first reading of the bill took place in the Riigikogu on 31 May 2021. No amendments were tabled by the deadline of 3 June at 10 a.m. The Legal Affairs Committee asked about the draft opinion of the Supreme Court, county and district courts, the Public Prosecutor's Office, the Estonian Bar Association, the Office of the Chancellor of Justice and the Faculty of Law of the University of Tartu. Pärnu County Court, Public Prosecutor's Office, Harju County Court, Estonian Bar Association, Supreme Court, Tartu County Court, Viru County Court, Estonian Information Technology and Telecommunications Association submitted opinions to the Legal Affairs Committee. The Committee on Legal Affairs prepared the draft for today's second reading at three meetings: on 3, 10 and 14 June. At the meeting of the Committee on Legal Affairs held on 3 June, the chairman of the committee, Jaanus Karilaid, proposed that the opinions on the draft be heard at this meeting of the committee. There were nine opinions on the draft, as I said, five of which were more critical or included proposals. Tim Kolk, the representative of the Supreme Court, said that the draft could eliminate a narrow contradiction in the interpretation of the caselaw of the Court of Justice of the European Union between Article 15 (1) of Directive 2002/58 and § 901 (2) of the Code of Criminal Procedure in so far as it provides for the consultation of communications data with the permission of the prosecutor's office. At the same time, the Directive remains partly inconsistent with §1111 of the Electronic Communications Act and some related provisions which provide for the obligation to keep communication data in general. Markus Kärner, Deputy Chancellor of Criminal Policy at the Ministry of Justice, said that the problem of maintaining communication data is the most complex and frequent issue in the case law of the Court of Justice of the European Union. Indeed, the judgments of the Court of Justice of the European Union are subject to restrictions according to which communication data must not be stored without distinction. Nearly 18 Member States of the European Union have so far failed to limit their own data retention regulations. In the future, this issue will certainly need to be re-examined. The representative of ITL, the Estonian Information Technology and Telecommunications Association, Kristiina Maasik, considered the draft to be a step in the right direction, but according to ITL, the requirement to retain communications data is a problem. Mrs Maasik also noted that a specific draft does not provide sufficient legal clarity as to when serious crimes are committed and when requests for communication data are permitted under the draft. The judgment of the Court of Justice of the European Union makes it clear that communication data can be used to combat serious crime and to prevent a serious threat to public policy. Paragraph 1111 (11) of the Electronic Communications Act referred to above, which creates a broad catalogue, is also in question. On this basis, many institutions can make inquiries outside criminal proceedings, starting with the Environmental Board and ending with, for example, the Financial Supervision Authority. Advisor to the Legal Affairs Committee Carina Rikart introduced the opinion of the Pärnu County Court. In its opinion, Pärnu County Court has dealt with § 901 (4) of the Code of Criminal Procedure in its original draft, which provides, instead of "serious crime and its nature', that requests for communication data covered by § 1111 (2) and (3) of the Law on electronic communications may be made only to persons suspected or accused of preparing or committing a criminal offence referred to in § 126 (2) of the Code of Criminal Procedure and to persons directly involved in the act of suspicion or charge. Paragraph 126 (2) of the Code of Criminal Procedure refers to the criminal offences covered by the Penal Code, in respect of which surveillance operations can be carried out. The Pärnu County Court has proposed adding a new paragraph 5 of § 901 of the Code of Criminal Procedure, which proposes the most important points that must be included in the permit. As a matter of principle, the judge should be instructed on the basis of which the authorisation can be granted. This is in particular the volume of data, the period of data to be collected and the purpose of the operation. Ms Astrid, President of the Harju County Court, said that Harju County Court had an additional remark that the explanatory memorandum to the draft had to be supplemented by an addition to the implementation acts needed to be amended. Since the number of judges is provided for in the Regulation of the Minister of Justice, an increase in the number of judges should also provide for an amendment of that Regulation. He said that, in other respects, he maintained his previous position that it was necessary to provide either for a specific catalogue of the seriousness of the crime in which the act may be carried out, or to exclude the "gravity and nature of the crime" from the draft text at all. Elmer Muna, the representative of the Estonian Bar Association, also welcomed the criticism of the draft by the courts and, from the point of view of the Court of Justice of the European Union, it is mainly a matter of guaranteeing the fundamental rights of people. The Court of Justice of the European Union has clearly criticised the fact that communication data may not be collected or stored in this way and that communication data permits may not be issued in this way. The leading state prosecutor, Taavi Pern, said that the prosecutor's office supported the bill. The draft resolves at least the problem that the prosecutor's office is unable to continue with the authorisation of calls at the moment. Mr Pern also said that it was reasonable that the problem should be dealt with in part. The Chair of the Committee on Legal Affairs, Jaanus Karilaid, proposed to continue the debate in the Committee on Legal Affairs on 10 June and to set up a working group with the participation of those who have submitted the above-mentioned proposals and opinions. By the meeting on 10 June, this working group had prepared the original amendment, but I will present the final amendment rather than to the minutes of 14 June. Ms Rikart presented what had happened in the working group and said that the group had a strong view that the list of crimes for which requests for communication data could be made should be narrowed. The working group based its views on the Pärnu County Court, which also refers to the crime catalogue, where surveillance activities can be carried out and which would also justify requests for communication data. At the same time, there is also a compromise proposed by Astrid, Chairman of the Harju County Court, that, in the case of crimes not covered by § 126 (2) of the Code of Criminal Procedure, the possibility of making requests for communication data is exceptional if justified by the gravity and nature of the crime and if the request does not unduly infringe the rights of persons and is indispensable for the purpose of criminal proceedings. This proposal, too, has been further rewritten that the order of the court must contain the data to be collected by the request, the justification for the collection of the data and the period for which the data are requested. The committee then held a debate where representatives of both the Ministry of Justice and the Ministry of the Interior stressed that they would rather support the initial version of the draft. The Commission decided that we would give the working group another weekend to review the clarifications and wording and come back to the final amendment to be finalised by the Committee on Legal Affairs at its meeting on 14 June. We then needed to decide on a proposal to discuss this draft at second reading on 15 June and to ask the Bureau to change the time when documents were available. Jaanus Karilaid suggested that the Bureau be allowed to submit documents by 5 p.m. on 14 June. It was unanimously supported by the Commission. Since I am running out of time, I will say briefly that on 14 June...
ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-16_U202	There's almost ten minutes left.
ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-16_U203	Oh, I was wrong about our time. Then I will present this amendment to you calmly. By 14 June, the working group had indeed made its corrections and I will present the new, amended proposal. The amendment amends § 901 (2) and now paragraph 2 looks as follows: "The investigating authority may, at the request of the prosecutor's office and with the permission of the investigating judge in pre-trial proceedings or with the permission of the court, request the electronic communications undertaking to consult the electronic communications company on the data listed in paragraphs 2 and 3 of § 1111 of the Electronic Communications Act other than those mentioned in subsection 1 of this section." Paragraph 3 reads as follows: "The request provided for in subsection 2 of this section may be made in the case of a criminal offence referred to in subsection 2 of § 1262 of this Code and where this is strictly necessary to achieve the purpose of criminal proceedings. In the case of offences not listed in the list, a request for communication data shall be permitted where this is strictly necessary for the purpose of criminal proceedings and justified by the gravity and nature of the offence and where the request does not unduly prejudice personal rights.'; Paragraph 4: "The request permission shall indicate: 1) the data which may be collected by the request; 2) the justification for the collection of the data; 3) the period for which data may be collected." Paragraph 5 states that "[t]he order of the investigating judge and the order of the court to rule on the application of the prosecutor's office may, at the request of the prosecutor's office, be drafted as a headline.' and paragraph 6: "In cases of urgency where it is not possible to formalise an investigative judge's or court's authorisation in due time, the request referred to in paragraph 2 of this section may be made on the basis of the permission of the prosecutor's office, given in a manner which allows for reproduction and which contains at least the information set out in subsections 1 and 3 of subsection 4 of this section. In such a case, a reasoned request to the court must be made within the first working day following the request for authorisation. The investigating judge shall decide on the admissibility of the request by means of a regulation which may have been drafted as a headline at the request of the prosecutor's office. "This is the content of the amendment accepted by the Commission. The Commission also decided, at its meeting on 14 June, that the issues that remained open to the collection of electronic communications data in the wider sense of the Directive would be returned in the autumn and that changes to the Electronic Communications Act would probably also be needed. That's all I have right now. Thank you.

The Court of Justice of the European Union found that, in this respect, the prosecutor is not an independent judge; he cannot ask for and publish for himself. As a result, the independence of legal proceedings in a trial suffers.

This is a two-fold process where the prosecutor first has to make his request and justify it and consider it in the same way and find that, for example, it is a serious crime. Then, in turn, the judge will have to decide whether the prosecutor's request is justified or not, whether it is a serious criminal offence of the kind in which it is precisely the use of communication data that is necessary for the successful conduct of criminal proceedings. For example, it is time-critical to obtain this information in order to proceed with criminal proceedings. In that case, the justification must be given on the working day following that on which the authorisation was granted. So no one's giving up the recital. However, for the time being, this authorisation is issued with the same two points that it is necessary to define the data to be collected and, secondly, the period for which the data are requested and that justification must be submitted to the Court within the next working day. And then the judge decides whether or not he actually accepts it and whether or not this information can be used in court proceedings.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-16_U294	Reuben Kaalep, please! Eight minutes in total, please.
ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-16_U295	Honourable Riigikogu! This is a very important issue. First of all, I must commend my previous speakers, Henn Põlluaasa, Peeter Ernitsa and Paul Puustusmaa, who very well summed up this round of topics from different facets. And maybe it would be difficult for me to add something to them. In the past, however, we did not have a very interesting discussion on history. However, with the urge and demand of the audience, I would continue to give us a very important and inspiring overview of how unemployment was fought in Estonia in the 1920s and 1930s. Emergency work organised by municipalities and public authorities and, to a small extent, by private companies became the dominant tool to combat unemployment. They were organised between November and April in a period where unemployment was the highest, since there was a break in seasonal work in agriculture, construction and certain industries (brickstone, lime, cement, sawmills). As the state did not have the means to organise emergency work to the extent that all jobseekers registered on the job exchange could be employed, the order of placement was determined. To this end, the unemployed were classified into three categories. The first was the male and female unemployed who had a family to support. In the second category, the individual female and male unemployed who were dependent on a spouse were classified. The third category consisted of a few male unemployed women and married women whose spouse was able to work. Priority was given to work for the unemployed in the first category. In the event that the amount of work allowed it, the second and third categories of unemployed persons were to be put into employment. In practice, employment for the last two categories of unemployed was close to zero due to the small volume of emergency work. In December 1925, there was a locality censorship to get into emergency work. Only unemployed people who had maintained an apartment in the city for at least one year could be assigned to emergency work. Individuals and those who had no survivors were advised not to be employed at all by the Committee on Combating Unemployment. These requirements narrowed down already the opportunities for employment through work exchanges. From January to May 1925, the average number of jobs in emergency work was 51.7%, in the same period in 1926 37.9% and in 1927 34.2% of the total number of jobseekers registered on job exchanges. However, most applications for employment were rejected. Since relief work mainly involved soil work, it was mainly men who found use there. The government of Jaan Tõnisson, the State Elder, who took office in December 1927, considered increasing the employment rate of residents through economic development as one of its priorities. On 12 January 1927, the Head of State called for a broad-based consultation in order to determine the possibilities for the development of the various spheres of the economy and the creation of new jobs. The members of the government and the Riigikogu, representatives of the Chamber of Commerce and Industry, farmers and cities and others took part. At the meeting, the idea remained that the import of imported goods and the production of the same goods in Estonia should be restricted, as far as possible, to increase export production, to grant public orders to Estonian companies, to restructure the activities of labour exchanges, to place greater emphasis on vocational education and the like. However, the development of concrete measures was not achieved. In the summer of 1929, officials of the Ministry of Labour and Welfare visited Latvia, Poland, Austria, Denmark and Germany to familiarise themselves with the activities of labour exchanges in other countries and with the measures taken to reduce unemployment. The high economics prevailing on the world market in the second half of the 1920s enabled Estonia's industrial and agricultural exports to increase significantly, giving the opportunity to create new jobs. Therefore, there was no need for special measures other than the traditional emergency work during the spring winter. In 1930, when the global economic crisis began to affect the Estonian economy and as a result of the contraction of production, the number of unemployed registered on the labour market increased rapidly, the state increased the amounts for emergency work, but also made the conditions for access to emergency work more stringent. According to the Circular of the Ministry of Education and Social Affairs of 26 November 1930, only unemployed people who were citizens of the Republic of Estonia could be sent to emergency work by the labour exchanges for at least one year and worked for at least 12 weeks during the last year, except for emergency work, without working for at least one month. If the unemployed was married, the spouse's income per month was not allowed to exceed 25 kroons. Due to the limited volume of emergency work, the job placement was preferred to the unemployed, who were supported by underage children and unable family members. The provisions laid down by the Ministry affected the labour market as a whole, and in particular the Narva region, where 15 to 20% of registered jobseekers were stateless. Since a large number of protest letters were received against the circular of the Ministry restricting access to emergency work, the Ministry softened the rules by allowing exceptions to stateless persons who lived in Estonia for at least 10 years. One measure to alleviate unemployment was to reduce working time in industrial plants to eight hours a day from 1 September 1931. This step was intended to increase employment by forcing workers to hire additional workers to cope with all the necessary jobs. However, a rather drastic measure was the Act on the organisation of service relationships for the purpose of combating unemployment, which entered into force on April 1, 1932, which provided that if both spouses are employed in a state or local government institution, including an educational institution, a woman must be dismissed if her husband earns at least EEK 140 per month in the city and 80 per month in the country. One person was banned from working in a state or self-government institution or in two fixed-wage jobs in an enterprise. The Act also authorised the dismissal of persons entitled to a pension from the state and local government service who had earned an old-age pension. The worrying situation at the beginning of 1932 forced a thorough analysis of the state of play and a search for escape routes. The Estonian Society of Economists announced a competition for competitions on "Extent of work, nature and possibilities for solutions in Estonia." Two of the eight works received by 15 May 1932, Richard Ring and August Gutmann's work, were judged worthy of the second prize and published as a book with the joint financial resources of the Ministry of Education and Social Affairs, the Ministry of Economic Affairs, the Chamber of Commerce and Industry, the Conjuncture Office and the Estonian Society of Economicsologists. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-16_U336	Thank you, Mr. Chairman. Ladies and gentlemen, Good morning. It's a new day. Let's keep going. You have before you a draft law amending the General Part of the Environmental Code and other laws. This concerns, in particular, the repeal of the Environmental Register Act and the creation of better solutions instead. The draft law amending the General Part of the Environmental Code and other laws was initiated by the Government of the Republic on 23 November 2020, but the preparation was indeed already carried out when Rene Koka was Minister of the Environment. The draft will update the procedures for handling environmental information in the databases and create an environmental portal bringing together environmental data. We have quite a few of these drafts. This indicates that the information society really needs to be implemented in law and that such an Estonian nature information system is created. So far, it has operated as sub-information systems in the environmental register, and now it is creating a single IT point of access to and interface with databases containing environmental information, which should be a "one-stop-shop" for a person, in plain language. There is a web environment that brings together all environmental data. The first reading of the draft decision was completed on 17 February, including. No amendments were tabled by the deadline, but there was quite a long and thorough work to be done by the Commission. That's why the time has gone. As a result of the Commission's work, it was decided to table 11 Commission amendments on the draft, which we will certainly be able to address here today. The Commission met on 22 February, 3 March, 23 March, 13 April, 1 June and 3 June this year for the preparation of the second reading. At the beginning of March, we had a proposal from the Ministry of the Environment to supplement the draft provisions with different laws: the law on atmospheric air protection, the Waste Act, the Industrial Emissions Act and the Water Act. On the basis of these, registrations will be made and will also be included in the environmental permit information system KOTKAS. You could say that KOTKAS is the main focus of this draft. On 1 June, it was precisely in the Commission that a position was drawn up on these proposals and it was decided to include them as an amendment by the Commission. The decision was taken in a consensual manner. On 13 April, a discussion was held on environmental information collection and management issues under the administration of the Ministry of the Environment. The [representatives] of the Ministry of the Environment and the Environment Agency were present as well as a consultant involved in the preparation of the draft. At subsequent meetings, the proposals sent to the Commission by the Ministry concerning the provisions of the Forest Act were discussed and the two topics that were in the air concerned the forest register and the statistical forest inventory. There were also quite a number of interest groups who approached our committee. To begin with, I will briefly clarify what has been asked about and why we in the Commission decided today to call the Minister as co-rapporteur. This is an important issue in the area of the environment, which concerns all forest information on our environment. Statistical forest inventory is a system in which Estonia has since 1999 been divided into a grid consisting of plots measuring the condition of our forest, such as the type of trees, the number of flatwood, the size of the increment, the amount of cut. There are a number of other aspects that are being examined. Some of these plots have been permanently one and the same since 1999 and others have been rotating or randomly selected new each year. A similar system is used in many parts of the world and on the basis of its statistical forest inventory, Estonia also provides its data for example in the LULUCF accounts for emissions trade in the land use sector. In recent times, some scientists and conservationists have been interested in finding out whether this forest statistics are reliable, and they have made inquiries during the whole debate on logging in society. The Environmental Agency, which is responsible for this SMI system, has found that the data on these permanent plots, if they were to issue these locations, would present a risk that these data would no longer be reliable. Even if no one goes there because of this knowledge to do more or less cutting, this risk in itself reduces this reliability in relation to these data and the Environment Agency has not issued this data. However, in turn, the Data Protection Inspectorate, who was also one of the parties involved in the Commission's proceedings, made an injunction and stated that it was necessary to determine in a special law why and under what conditions a restriction on access to these permanent sample locations would be imposed. That was one of the topics we discussed at length. We had scientists from the Estonian University of Life Sciences who were very concerned about the fact that, if this restriction on access is not imposed, the statistics are no longer reliable. On the contrary, scientists at the University of Tartu who were on the Commission once again made it very clear that researchers must have access to these data in order to carry out forestry work. The same view was taken by the representatives of the Estonian Nature Foundation, who were also present at the Commission in the proceedings. Finally, after long discussions on this issue, we reached such a compromise, and the Members of the Commission were all of the opinion that researchers must have access to the data, and an amendment has been tabled to this end, which says that under a data-use agreement, scientists must obtain this data for their own use of forestry research. I think it was very important, and it will actually ensure that it is possible to obtain information on whether this method of inventory is reliable, because it is the basis for a great many forestry decisions and if this so-called micro-infringement, as expressed by one Member of the Commission, is inside it, it will be of no use to anyone. This basis, which is created here today by these amendments, for researchers to receive this data, really allows independent parties to audit the SMI system itself as well. The second part of the amendments, over which there was a longer debate in the Commission, concerned the forest register. The Estonian Private Forest Association and the Private Forest Centre participated in the discussion and the problem of data that is visible in the forest register arose there. According to this new solution, many data will be included in the publicly visible data. However, the data which the Ministry treats as personal data, i.e. the data by which the size of the forest owner's assets can be estimated, such as the volume of felling in this particular forest land it owns, will no longer be immediately available. One of the reasons for this is that private forest owners are often approached by such automatic or robot inquiries, that certain hectares or areas with a certain volume of felling are taken out and that there are very strong sales offers. Originally, the Ministry's proposal was that it would have been applicable to both legal and self-employed forest owners, but as a result of the Commission's discussions, we found that legal persons are engaged in economic activities and that this is in no way deplorable for them. As a compromise, there was a proposal that forest owners could be free in their activities and maybe in the future the problem with these so-called raccoons, as is the name of the matter. Since this is indeed a rather specific draft, we also have a minister as a co-rapporteur today, but of course I am also ready to answer your questions. Thank you.

It may be [confused] if it is not dealt with on a daily basis, and I think it is also one of the reasons why it has raised many questions. This SMI methodology is the same statistical forest inventory methodology where Estonia is divided into pieces and where these are permanent and temporary pieces. While the locations of the permanent pieces on which changes in the forest are observed on the same specific pieces of time, the location of which is known only to a very small number of people, should become public, the point of Meelis Seedre's comment was that this statistics are no longer reliable because it could be manipulated by the knowledge made on these specific pieces. For example, whether you chop more or not to chop on this piece, plant some type of wood or other. These are all, of course, theoretical installations, but it is considered that this statistical principle, that there is such anonymity, must be ensured so that this methodology cannot be called into question.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-16_U461	Dear Riigikogu! Why has this draft raised disputes? Because it is possible to restrict the access of scientists and experts to statistical forest inventory data. Why is this important? Because the statistical forest inventory provides answers to a question that is important for the future of our country: is the forest being cut too much or too little in Estonia? What does the statistical forest inventory have to do with this? The fact that there are contradictions between its data, which are precisely about this fundamental issue. Let me give you an example. On 3 June s. A new statistical forest inventory was published, according to which 11.25 million cubic metres of forest were cut in Estonia in 2019 and 10,6 million cubic metres in 2020. According to the Environmental Agency, in 2019 the net growth of the economic forest was over 12 million cubic metres. A year earlier, the felling volume was 12.74 and a year before 12.5 million cubic metres. According to recent data from the SMI, 3.9 million cubic metres of timber were cut off from state land and 7.3 million cubic metres were cut off from private land. Since private ownership is slightly less than half the forest land, these figures show that the private forest was cut twice as intensively as the state forest in 2019. At the same time, forest assets have fallen according to the same SMI. In 2019, Estonia had 481 million cubic metres of forest assets. By 2020, it had shrunk to 472 million cubic metres. Thus, the timber assets of the Estonian stands have decreased by 9 million cubic metres in one year, i.e. the net growth of the Estonian forest was less than 9 million cubic metres. There is a clear difference in data for which there is no simple explanation. This cannot be explained, for example, by the expansion of protected areas, as has been done inexplicably, because it is not taken into account in the assessment of timber assets. It can only be said that a net increase of 12 million cubic metres and a reduction of 9 million cubic metres cannot be true at the same time. Unfortunately, on these issues, unexplained anomalies and unclear information, much of the misunderstandings about the ecological future of the Estonian forest and the future prospects of the forestry sector lie. These two do not necessarily have to contrast with each other if we can bring clarity to unambiguous facts from which decisions affecting the Estonian environment can be made. However, it is urgently necessary for at least expert ecologists and scientists to have access to the basic data from which these numbers are derived. So far, the Environmental Agency, which is subordinate to the Ministry of the Environment, has simply refused to issue data on the location of permanent plots. Recently, the Data Protection Inspectorate decided that this was still public data and that it was therefore obliged to submit this data to the requesting parties, including the University of Tartu. On the other hand, § 91 of the Forest Act, which is planned by this Bill, allows the data to be left for internal use, unless the Environmental Agency itself agrees to share them with scientists. This, in turn, means that the sharing of data with researchers is essentially made dependent on the discretion of environmental agency officials. The environmental agency will have the right to refuse to conclude a data-use contract if it is not convinced that the data will be used exclusively for research in the forestry sector and that they will not be provided to outsiders. The emphasis is on the word 'convinced' because it is quite unprecedented that the availability of such vital data to experts in their field who are able to evaluate them is made dependent on the conviction of officials. The justification is the protection of personal data, which is prima facie necessary and a noble angle of approach. More time, please!

But the problem is that an unusually important change was introduced into this draft after the first reading as proposals by the Ministry of the Environment. Their proposals have not been discussed with stakeholders and environmental organisations; they were not discussed with the Data Protection Inspectorate. There is no doubt that this way of proposing and processing changes is not correct, and that is what has caused the most controversy. I will also focus on these issues in my speech. Let's take these two issues apart if they even tend to go through here. Regulation of statistical forest inventory data. In general, no one in this Parliament has called for all statistical forest inventory data to be fully public to everyone. We understand this as regards the impact of the disclosure of the locations of permanent plots on the reliability of these data. They just don't give them, they play dumb, literally. It is said that you will submit an application, you will submit applications, you will now process them for three months, and then you will simply refuse, you will not say anything, but you will not be issued. This is the real situation we've been in. This wording of itself could be something to move forward if the majority of the Commission, rather than implementing the roller, had actually analysed these provisions and agreed on them. But it wasn't done. Well, the chairman of the committee agreed with the minister there, but there was no substantive debate on these points in the committee. This wording is poor. Unfortunately, it's bad. I'll explain why. Firstly, the provision that has been proposed. Indeed, if you read these provisions in conjunction, the Environmental Agency will have the right to issue permanent rehearsal pieces [data] in certain limited cases, but the obligation to outsource them to ecologists at the University of Tartu, for example, does not provide for this law, because the basic provision contains the word "may." Well, I can give it to you, but I can't give it to you. I proposed that we take the word'may' out, i.e. must be issued, unless there are some exclusionary conditions. This amendment was not accepted. Secondly, the grounds for refusal set out in the same provision. It was already said today that it would not be issued. The Environment Agency must refuse if it is not convinced that whoever wants the data will actually use them for the right purpose and so on. Well, it is not a correct legal text in a situation of mistrust. Past practice has shown that the availability of these data is a problem for researchers of the University of Tartu who are completely reliable. This conviction has so far been held by the Environmental Agency that they will not provide this data. This provision does not correct this problem, which it should correct. The third question, which was not analysed in the Commission at all, because it really went so far, is that they are issued for research in the field of forestry. According to the Estonian language dictionary, forestry is an industry and related applied science that covers the management and use of forests. Forest ecology is not forestry as interpreted by the Estonian Language Institute. Of course, it is possible and logical to interpret it more widely. Mr. Hepner shakes his head, so did I. I asked the Minister extra to confirm that the ecologists of the University of Tartu also have access to these data. He couldn't confirm it. Couldn't confirm. Why are we making laws where we really can't predict what their applied effects are? The chairman of the committee says that maybe before the third reading, something can be improved. Well, the Rules of Procedure, unfortunately, do not allow anything to be improved until the third reading. In essence, the reading of the draft must be interrupted in order to actually make these changes. Now, the topics of the forest register concerning the concealment of certain forest information about the forest property of natural persons. The principle is very simple: the Environmental Information Directive, the Aarhus Convention and the general constitutional framework are that environmental information is public, unless there are reasons to classify it or to restrict access to it. If this restriction on access is applied, there must be clear grounds for doing so, and it must take place in the minimum necessary way, not all of it immediately, but precisely what is necessary in order to protect this other interest. This other interest is, and it has been said, that there should be no aggressive sales offers, robot inquiries, forest owners, etc. There's a lot of problems opening up here. The Data Protection Inspectorate in the Commission also expressed its view, myself and many other parties, that this issue could be resolved in a less restrictive way. It is possible, for example, to rule out information technology robot requests, but it is also possible to make these data passively public, that is to say that they cannot be downloaded from the register, but they can be retrieved by submitting a request for information when we are talking about solving the problem of raccoons. All these issues were rejected without a substantive proportionality assessment, an adequate proportionality assessment, which is crucial from a constitutional point of view. Impacts have also not been assessed more widely. The Minister could not say today, for example, how many pieces of forest or plot of land, a piece of forest, for example. The explanatory memorandum states that there are pieces of wooded land with both a private owner and a legal owner whose records are closed by 2000. However, there are no data provided on the number of persons who are only natural persons. Not to mention the effects of this provision on environmental objectives by environmental organisations on behalf of all of us. So actually, I admit that we may need to adjust certain data in the forest register, to solve this problem, although I very much agree with Mr Seedri, who said I was sorry, but why not farmland. I'd look more broadly: we have buildings, residential buildings, real estate, everything in the register public. You can see where your apartment is, when it was built, basically its value, and make you a purchase offer. Then we need to look at this frame much more broadly, systematically, if we want to do some kind of restraint here. In such a fast-track way, through the back door of the second bill so fundamental changes are unacceptable. This is why the second reading of this draft must be interrupted and further discussed in the autumn. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-16_U519	Thank you. I still don't think it's right to burn wood in the kettle. And that can't be the case in the long run. Our views have been very different in this regard, and we had very thorough debates on this subject. The main concern is, firstly, whether there will be pressure to cut more wood to use in Auvere and, secondly, what problem we will actually solve. The compromise is that we have a fair transfer fund that provides money for the construction of a cogeneration plant in Narva in order to overcome the constant problem of Narva's heat and that we do not have any socio-economic concerns there. It will take time to build this cogeneration plant, and in the meantime there will be pressure on the price of Narva's heat, as the production of energy by using oil shale is very carbon intensive. Since the energy produced in this energy block also has to buy a CO2 emission quota and it has recently made a very high price increase, the combined production of electricity and heat is no longer profitable and the price in Narva is rising. At the moment, the compromise is that only waste wood can be used. And I've got those prices right here. In fact, this does not mean that more timber will be cut. Secondly, there is a limit for two years, precisely to create a cogeneration plant to provide heat to Narva. Thirdly, the permissible diameter of the wood waste is fixed. As the subsidy has a price cap of EUR 10 Megawatt-hour, a ceiling of 500 gigawatt-hours and a time limit of two years and a State aid permit has been granted, this grant will only allow the use of the lowest priced fuel, i.e. waste wood with a minimum price of EUR 7,98 per megawatt-hour and the highest price of EUR 12,9 per megawatt-hour. This means that no wood other than what is left over can be used at this price. But [after] I can continue to answer.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-16_U565	Thank you, good manager. Prime Minister! Under the current law, a natural person has the right to deduct from the income the interest on the loan or lease of a dwelling or a dwelling taken as a dwelling. Interest on a loan or lease of only one dwelling may be deducted at the same time. If the dwelling is co-owned (i.e. the dwelling has two owners) and both persons are also loan applicants, the interest on the mortgage can be declared and deducted from the income both in proportion to the share of the property. In the case of loans taken from 2005 onwards, interest may be deducted from income only if the dwelling has been purchased as a place of residence. Today we are going to discuss here the draft law amending the Income Tax Act, which will abolish this right for our homeowners. And here's my question: why do you want to take away from our homeowners the right and the possibility to deduct from your taxable income interest on a loan or lease for a dwelling or a dwelling?
ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-16_U566	Thank you. Such a mortgage interest tax credit was adopted in 1996. At the time, bank interest rates were really very high, 12% to 13%. The purpose of this advantage was for people to be able to buy a home in such a way that the capital market failure would not be in the form of excessively high interest rates behind which the purchase of their home would remain. In 1997, the [credit interest] rate was 12 to 13%, but now, in 2021, the interest rate is 2%. Perhaps this market failure will no longer exist. People get loans from banks on perfectly reasonable terms. Thus, the obstacle to obtaining housing is no longer a high borrowing rate that the state should contribute to, but something else. And actually, if we are looking at the real estate market now, there are no obstacles, rather it is estimated that the real estate market is overheating because everyone acquires a home. So, today, these interest rates have fallen over and no longer need state support, no need to use taxpayers' money to support these interest payments. This change will apply to next year's revenue. From now on, this deduction limit will apply only to training costs and donations. Again, the purpose of the tax credit is for people to make more donations. There is also lifelong learning and the deduction of training costs is therefore quite reasonable. I would also like to say that the OECD has also recommended the abolition of the housing loan interest tax credit tax credit because it benefits households with higher incomes in particular, i.e. it does not help people who are less financially insured, and thus increases inequality.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-16_U586	Thank you. Paragraph 54 of the Constitution concerns that everyone has a duty to be loyal to the constitutional order and to defend Estonia's independence. "I suggest you read through the constitutional comments, this whole thick edition. It also lists what constitutional order means. The constitutional order of the Republic of Estonia is that we have free elections, resulting in the formation of 101 members of the Riigikogu. These 101 members have been elected in free elections. The Riigikogu elects the government, gives it a mandate. If the government has received a mandate in accordance with the Constitution, this government is legitimate. The constitutional order also includes that we have jurisdiction in the hands of the courts and that we have separation of powers. So these are different elements of constitutional order. What you're talking about, you've put together a lot of interesting things or different sections of the Constitution. Paragraph 124, to which you refer, concerns defence. Defence is a military service and everything to do with it. In that sense, these things aren't exactly related. Now, fundamental rights or constitutional order, I hope that you will also read the paragraphs that talk about constitutional order, which is exactly what it is. But the second aspect you pointed out is the right of a person to his or her life and health. In paragraph 16, [the letter that everyone has] the right to life is protected by law. No one must be tortured, cruelly or demeaningly treated, or punished, as well as the paragraph you referred to. These are people's fundamental rights, I'm not going to read them all about the Constitution. According to the Constitution, the people of Estonia have these rights, but not only the people of Estonia, but in fact, such principles are generally applicable to almost all parts of the world.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-16_U591	Thank you, Mr President. Good Prime Minister! I'd like to find out. ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-16_U592	Thank you. Look, the constitution works as a whole. The rights of a person can be limited in order to protect other people. A good colleague, Helmen Kütt, has also pointed out here in the Riigikogu that if on one side there is a person's right to life and on the other side there are employer's rights or employee's rights, where does this balance go? Vaccination is and will remain voluntary, but in the context of a risk assessment each employer must assess the nature, size and duration of the risk of infection by his staff member and must also take safety measures to prevent or reduce its impact. The employer cannot oblige the worker to go into vaccination, i.e. the worker has the right to refuse vaccination. Since workers cannot and must not be forced to vaccinate, as this is contrary to the principle of physical integrity of a person, if this hazard analysis or risk analysis cannot eliminate the risk by other means or by reducing the risk of health to the lowest possible level, it is important that the worker knows the consequences in the employer's view. If a person refuses to vaccinate, he or she should be given or offered a different job. It's like any other working condition that if this job doesn't suit you, you can't do this job. You'll be offered a different job with the same employer. But if there's no offer, what do we do? A person has the right to life and health. But the person who gets hospitalized because he has a weak immune system, his life, in any case, outweighs the fact that [in an institution] someone wants to work without vaccination, [in association with] people who can get sick. So in order not to have serious cases and not to have deaths, such safety measures are necessary.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-16_U667	Thank you. Good Riigikogu! Listening to all this, I feel like a real giant, everything that happens around here is my doing. But it doesn't seem to me to match the narrative that I'm weak when I'm so strong. When I took office at the end of January, I realized that the situation with the state budget might be bad, but in such a bad situation, I never dreamt of having a state budget. The major surprise concerned the years 2022 to 2024, for which the current national budget strategy provided for a deficit of 5.4% [first], 4.3% [second] and 2.5% [third year]. Those numbers were startlingly big. Later it turned out that they were actually even bigger because they did not include the billion-euro bluff that had been written into it. It was surprising how irresponsiblely and lightly the promises of the future had been shared. At the end of 2020, the general government general budget expenditure exceeded revenue by EUR 1.3 billion, with central government expenditure exceeding revenue by EUR 991 million. Consolidated government debt was close to EUR 5 billion by the end of the year. Compared to 2019, the debt had doubled. It was also the highest figure in the last 20 years. Crisis measures were implemented in the governments of the EKRE and of the Fatherland in such a way that the link between the crisis and the damage could not be established. For example, in several cases, the MES has distributed credit to companies intended to mitigate the effects of the COVID-19 crisis, which itself did not notice the effects of the crisis or merely saw the bank not lending them money as an effect. Similarly, when issuing loans, the MES often did not make it clear whether the credit needs had been caused by the crisis in COVID. During the audit, the National Audit Office also found that the foundation has treated loan applicants differently, asking some companies for more detailed information than others. Why am I talking about this? I speak because all this was done by the initiators of mistrust in the rule of the EKRE and the Fatherland. It was a starting line that I inherited in winter. When the state's budget strategy began to be dealt with, the billion-billion-bluff agreed by the previous government had to be resolved in the country's budget strategy, i.e. the promise to cut an average of more than 300 million euros in one year. That's five or six times as much as the government is now accused of. Today's distrusters EKRE and the Fatherland also know that the budgetary strategy they put together was a fraud. I quote the words of Martin Helme, then Minister of Finance, which appeared on 28 May in the Eesti Päevaleht: "I know you have a little secret that we put these cuts exactly as big as the deficits. So the table's zero. To make Excel beautiful. And no one took that minus seriously or that cut-off amount. It just didn't make sense." The politicians of the Isamaa were also aware that the RES, put together jointly with the EKRE, is aware of the confusion and confusion of the public, simply because it was also written on page 137 of the country's budget strategy. The Fatherland, however, pretended to be concerned about the financial situation of the Estonian state. At least Martin Helme did not pretend to want to cut the budget, and in that sense he is certainly more honest with his constituents. On the other hand, President of Isamaa Helir-Valdor Seeder told Äripäev on 11 September 2020 that moving by 0.5% towards fiscal balance was too loose a pace. But already on 29 September was approved with the legendary RES whose motto was that Excel would be beautiful. Head Urmas Reinsalu, Priit Sibul, Sven Sester and, of course, Chairman of the party Helir-Valdor Seeder! The party you led was in government for four years. Yes, it was the same four years where the budget deficit grew to a record level today. Today's minus is your responsibility. Empty cuts are your real policy, and your empty words of austerity are proof that words and deeds do not go together in any way. However, you began to charge the current government at the first moment to cut costs. Here are, for example, some headlines from the media. Priit Sibul says in the Isamaa press release of 30 April 2021: "In addition, the Isamaa Group would like to see the government come up with a cost reduction plan of EUR 50 million. With the additional budget, the state's expenditure will increase significantly. In a crisis situation, the state must come to the aid of people and businesses, but it must also keep an eye on public expenditure. " Sven Sester writes in the Estonian Päevaleht on 24 March: "It is important that the additional budget is not only looking for spending options, but also for austerity points. The private sector has already done so, and the previous government also foreseen cuts in the country's budget strategy. In a crisis situation, the public sector must show solidarity with the private sector." These are eloquent quotations because they show how comfortable you feel when you talk against your own decisions. First you let the state budget into a record minus, then you come up with a record bluff on cost savings and you confirm the RES with empty cuts. In reality, you did not agree on any substantial savings. You kept talking about responsible fiscal policies, but you didn't make any decisions during the four years. You're talking about cuts, you're demanding it from today's government, but have you thought about the consequences of your own decisions? Today, you accuse me and the current government of not continuing this bluff, being honest and trying to actually find savings. We're not doing things because Excel is beautiful and the table is zero, just like you used to. We control those pink dreams that you kindly promised, knowing from the beginning that the money the state doesn't have for these things. The government I run is fair. My government is not making a country's budget strategy with unsuited cuts, and we are not going to pretend. In the context of a growing economy and with the support of its part, we will reduce the budget deficit by more than EUR 500 million, because public finances will create better foundations for possible next setbacks and crisis preparation and will ensure a stronger economy and a more prosperous life. In this regard, unlike the previous government, there is an early increase in the payroll fund for the most priority areas, such as internal security workers and teachers and cultural workers. We are investing strongly in Estonia's future. Over the next four years, the Estonian economy will receive more than EUR 8 billion, or approximately EUR 2 billion per year, as government investment. Such large sums of money have never been invested in the Estonian economy. The main aim of the investments is to implement a green and digital turnaround that is crucial for Estonia's future. We don't play with taxes. We promised a tax break and, despite the wishes of the previous government, if economic growth is above 2%, I would remind you that it is now 5.4%, then excise duties will be increased. We're not going along with this request. It will remain as the EKRE and the Fatherland governments wanted: the sunset of the excise duty rate will remain in 2022 and we will not change it. The tax burden will remain in 33% of the region over the next four years. In 2021 the tax burden will be 35%. As far as economic development is concerned, it has to be said that the outlook for the Estonian economy has improved considerably in recent months. Economic growth in the first quarter of this year was 5.4% of GDP. Economic forecasts have rather been changed in ascending line. This morning Eesti Pank published its new forecast, which foresees an increase of 3.8 to 4.9% for next year. In the first quarter, household investment in housing increased by 13%, company investment in transport vehicles by 71%, buildings and structures by 7%, and machinery and equipment by 5%. Our government is not dealing with nepotism. Crisis measures and aid packages are based on the principle that we are helping those who have suffered the most. The government of EKRE and Isamaa planted money for those who were not affected by the health crisis, while we directed additional aid to support the tourism sector which was heavily hit by the crisis to EUR 17.7 million, and for the development and development of companies EUR 28 million. But now for the cost of defense. We have promised that defence costs will continue to exceed 2% of GDP, plus a defence investment programme and host state support. As a result, protection expenditure will be significantly higher in 2022 than it was this year at 59.4 million and will remain larger for the entire period of the country's budgetary strategy. On the basis of the RES, EUR 704.9 million has been planned for defence costs in 2022, as calculated by NATO. This year it was EUR 645 million, and by 2025 it will grow to EUR 712.1 million. Next year, defence costs will increase twice as fast as last year. Only once in the history of re-independence in Estonia has the cost of defence increased so much. If you look at the rhetoric of EKRE, as much as EKRE is basking, no Estonian party has done so in history. Now the mistrust of the Fatherland has embraced the style characteristic of the EKRE. What concerns me most during this six months is that the EKRE has broken our external relations and ruined more than I thought when I was in the opposition. In 2004, Lennart Meri said that access to NATO means that Estonia will never be left alone again and a small country can be equal to the big ones. Estonia's security is based on our own defence capabilities and strong alliances. The previous government was characterised by a solid pattern: members of the EKRE government insulted our allies and undermined allies. This behavior had to be explained and smoothed all the time. Both the Finnish Prime Minister, Sanna Marin, and the President of the United States, Joe Biden played their part. The pattern was consistent. Our friends in the rest of the world raised their eyebrows and wondered where Estonia had come from. For my government, NATO and allied relations with the United States are fundamental. I am just coming from the NATO summit in Brussels and I can assure you that NATO is strong and united. Collective protection works. My government has a plan, and it is to ensure strong alliances in NATO. During the previous government, Mart Helme questioned the US presidential elections, as well as the functioning of NATO. We confirmed strong alliances in NATO. The important thing is that we are an ally of the United States, and the United States is our most important ally. NATO membership is our security guarantee, in addition to our own defence capabilities. In sum, the EKRE's desire to distrust me is not surprising, because we are carriers of completely different values. But I was a little surprised by the fact that the Fatherland wants to be like the EKRE, even if the duality of its own party in government administration is revealed in doing so. I would like to thank the social democrats, with whom we have world-view differences in monetary policy, but thank you for not going along with this mistrust of the EKRE. Thank you! I'm ready to answer questions.
