Okay, now the bill to yourself. This draft was launched on 14 December last year and the main aim of this draft is to align the Electronic Communications Act with the Directive of the European Parliament and of the Council establishing the European Electronic Communications Code, the Communication Directive, the deadline for transposition of which was 21 December 2020. The first reading took place on 24 March and the deadline for submitting amendments, 8 April by Riigikogu members, political groups and other committees did not propose amendments. The draft procedure in Parliament was notified of the respective alliances, market participants and the draft opinion. It is also included in the draft map of market participants, Tele2 etc., including, for example, the NGO Internet Society Estonia, ITL, the Association of Information Technology and Telecommunications, the Association of Estonian Cities and Municipalities, Law Firms and the NGO Community for the benefit of the Community. Once again, after all the proposals on the draft map, the originator's position has been set out. The Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications also expressed its views on these opinions and presented proposals to the commission which helped to make this draft more legal at the limited time we acted. The Commission itself discussed this draft at its meeting of 4 May, 17 May and 7 June. When I arrive in time, I will try to discuss the minutes of these sittings as well, but our minutes are quite detailed and can be found on the Internet. So if anyone wants to go back to history later or for any other activity, they're gonna get those protocols out of there. All the meetings were, of course, attended by representatives of the initiator of the [draft]. On 4 May, we heard opinions and discussed concerns. Key issues may be: safety of communications networks, reliability of technology, compensation for replacement of 5G security, storage and use of communication data, frequency of market analysis, distance sales calls, deadlines for switching operators, setting minimum rates for frequency competitions, coverage of costs of number 1247, obligation to retransmission of television programmes, right of access to a building owned by a state or local government unit. Some of the topics received more attention, some a bit shorter, some we did not discuss further in the course of this draft. There was a long debate on 5G security and possible compensation, as well as on the frequency of the market analysis. We have made one change concerning the cost of bearing the number 1247. The Legal and Analytical Department of the Riigikogu found that what was written in the draft law is not constitutional, and that is why we had to include this point. This is in the amendments. We also improved the deadlines on the proposal of the Association of Estonian Cities and Municipalities to coordinate how to install 5G objects. At the meeting of the Economic Commission on 17 May, this is now the second of three sittings, a representative of the Ministry of Justice participated in addition to the representatives of the MKM. We also talked about the security of communications networks, compensation, market analysis and telephone number 1247. Markko Künnapu, representative of the Ministry of Justice, gave an overview of the state of play regarding the development of regulation on the storage and use of communication data. This does not concern this bill, just because it came up for discussion and, if I remember correctly, the NGO Estonian Internet Society asked us for it, and so was the review. It is probably up to the Committee on Legal Affairs to make this change, if I remember correctly. The amendments were discussed and voted on on 7 June. We have tabled five amendments. These were submitted by the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications and, between the two readings, they also went through language editing and the corresponding corrections have been made. With the amendments, the first is mostly technical in the short term. The second amendment is to exclude from the draft the obligation of a communication undertaking to guarantee free of charge [telephone number] 1247 calls, and that was the place where we discussed whether it would be possible to impose this obligation in the same way as 112. Of course, 112 [obligation] comes again from other agreements and the [second] directive. Let's be honest, there were opinions. This amount did not seem large at the beginning, but, as I mentioned, the legal and analysis department of our own house said that putting such an obligation in this way is not constitutional, and that is why we have corrected and we are taking this point out. The third amendment concerns legal clarity, as it was also a matter for the government. If you remember, I must have been on the remote about 14 months ago to give a speech on the same bill. The law was then passed in the Riigikogu, i.e. the law, and there was a debate in the government in the meantime, where the Chancellor of Justice found that before imposing the obligation to authorise the hardware or software of the communication network by the Government of the Republic, the power of attorney should be specified. That is why this is also a third proposal. These different powers of attorneys have been specified here. To this I can mention that it is once again online on this bill card. MKM has also sent 36 pages of material to this effect if anyone wishes to read. Amendment 4, as I mentioned, was also the subject of a debate by the Cities and Municipalities Association, and some grounds for refusal were added, for which the municipality could have the possibility to refuse to install 5G, for example, architectural inadequacy. I think it's a pretty reasonable attitude. The time limit for taking a decision shall be extended from one month to two months in order to allow for consultation of different authorities, where appropriate. Amendment 5 concerns the date of entry into force. The date of entry into force is July 15, 2021. Procedural decisions and proposals of the lead committee. The Committee on Economic Affairs proposed that the draft be placed on the plenary agenda of the Riigikogu for a second reading on 14 June and the second reading closed. When the second reading of the draft is closed, the Commission's proposal to put the draft on the plenary agenda of the Riigikogu for the third reading and to hold a final vote on 17 June. I think I can introduce what happened at the committee meetings in the course of my work. Overall, I mentioned the most important, and the Commission's protocols are quite comprehensive. I think that the great amount of time that we spent in the Commission dealing with this whole draft was spent listening to the concerns of market participants and the various concerns and discussing what we could do to ensure that the law that comes from the legislator is of sufficient quality and that we ourselves are satisfied with it. One of the concerns that we discussed at length and which we also recorded in the minutes was the possibility of a debate on compensation. I will try briefly to refer it to the question that if there is a switch from certain technologies or if it is said that certain technologies do not fit into any networks, some of the so-called market participants are more in trouble than others, and they have proposed that, in order for this debate to end faster and the transition to be smoother, the State should compensate them for the possible costs. There was also a longer debate on this in the Commission. We reached such an attitude that, in general, since the state often has situations where the state has concerns with either security, health or whatever higher interests, we turn to the Ministry of Finance and the Ministry of Justice, and perhaps also to the Minister of Public Administration, this list is not definitive, and we propose that we consider and discuss such a compensation mechanism, if, for example, a transfer is necessary for security or other purposes. It can be technological, it can be in any other field. It could also be health protection or whatever the higher interest is. This, in our opinion, would have to be worked out and obviously cannot be resolved within the framework of this draft. We also talked to MKM representatives, Raul Rik and their lawyer. Why this would be necessary is that sometimes it is the result of important changes that are important to the state, perhaps important to the public, or important to the public, in an unpredictable business environment, and the aim does not have to be that, if public authorities make any decisions and whoever is affected by them, they will find themselves in a situation where their only way out is to go to court with their own country. As a result, we have written it down and we are making proposals. This has nothing to do with a possible dispute over this particular draft. And even the existence of a compensation mechanism does not guarantee that the compensation will be paid. This may be a description of one of the issues concerning the security of 5G and our discussions in the Commission. I'm not going to read the bill myself, everybody has it. I'll see what's more important. I told you about the number 1247. In this regard, it is the case that the state uses this hotline, it also used it in the crisis, and later it combines different functions, and in the Commission we realized that the country needs to reserve this number. However, it is not constitutional to assume that this will be done free of charge, unless it is an emergency phone and it is still connected with functions. This assessment came to us from the Chancellery of the Riigikogu and we propose that it be deleted. Yeah, I think that's enough for now. ParlaMint-EE_2021-06-16_U1192	Honourable Riigikogu! This draft has reopened a very wide range of issues, and I would like to take this opportunity to further develop the same ideas that emerged during the debate on Bill 366. Indeed, this circle of topics concerns the interaction between technology and the 21st century society and the security threats that may arise, particularly in totalitarian regimes. In the case of the Chinese Red Communist regime, Huawei is not the only example. China, for example, is having a very interesting experiment this year on digital currencies, which will lay the foundations for the total control of society. In several Chinese cities, the authorities gave up tens of millions of digital currencies, known as Renminbi, free of charge, as so-called red New Year's Packages, which can be downloaded from a smartphone. In Beijing and Suzhou city alone, the free digital money lottery released 200,000 red boxes worth 200 yuan, making about $31. And it's not just an altruistic agenda behind it. By handing out these so-called red packets, the Chinese Government will have the opportunity to use new technology, which could soon lead to the introduction of the world's first complete digital currency and lay down future technical standards for analogous projects. It is also interesting in this experiment that the soon-to-be-introduced digital yuan is accompanied by the "best-before-date," after which the money in the account is programmed to be erased from the account. It will in the past make money-owners citizens merely money users whose consumption behaviour is entirely under the control and direction of the central government. The Chinese Central Bank would issue a digital yuan through commercial banks with the capacity to carry out customer background checks, and a digital yuan would be used through digital wallets, which would be essentially a licensed phone application of the Chinese Central Bank. Such a programmed currency, each size of which would have an identifiable owner, would be under the watchful eye of an almighty central bank capable of directing citizens' consumption behaviour not only in the role of the last resort but, among other things, in the role of the latter consumer. The introduction of the digital yuan will give the Central Bank of China the greater role that the smaller part of the Chinese economy represents in terms of foreign trade. Now, in order to expand this issue once again, I would like to highlight the technological sphere as one of the most philosophical thinkers Dmitri Orlov's thoughts. This is a Russian scientist, engineer and futurologist living in the United States, who published the book Shrinking the Technosphere in 2016. Two years ago, an interview appeared with him, which is to be heard in SoundCloud, where he said: "... there are systems that people have developed in spite of personal or group interests and that function as emergent intelligence. The technosphere is a whole of certain types of man-made, but out of the human will, systems that increasingly dominate human lives, being less and less man-controlled. [---] Standardisation of everything and everyone is a sine qua non for effective control of human society. As far as the migration crisis is concerned, the goal of once again matching the technosphere with the aim of doing everything, including human nature, is to standardise that there exists a certain "universe humanity" and that human rights are above basically everything. All must be treated in the same way, regardless of how their ancestors lived and the conditions inherent to a particular ethnic-cultural group. The technology places everything in a standardised group, in which everyone is forced to submit to the same bureaucratic procedure, that is the purpose of the technosphere. [---] The technosis is quite robust in the sense that it's difficult to lose. No one can do that. There's no stop button on the technosphere. It marches blindly until something catastrophic happens. Disasters are encoded in the technosphere because, although the technosphere has some intelligence (in terms of purpose), it has no broader intelligence. Simply put, the technosphere is an idiotic genius. It is unable to grasp the physical limits of its growth and spread. It lacks the means to estimate declining investment revenues from oil and gas operations, the use of the biosphere as a sink or any other problem that may arise. Nor does it perceive the limits of the technology, the main prerequisite of the tech-sphere, is that the new technology is always better than the old one and that more technology is better than less. Therefore, solving any problem in the technological sphere is equivalent to using more technology, even if the technology itself is the root cause of the problem. [---] There are endless possibilities for abuse of the system here. People can be led like sheep when they become passive. Any effective social rebellion is easy to suppress in technodystopia. However, there are also good aspects of preventing crime, for example. The negative aspects have a lot to do with what giant corporations can do. This is much more likely to happen in countries where the government is no longer able to control large companies, such as the United States. The U.S. government has only minimal power over Google or Facebook, which in some cases blend into a monolithic whole with a federal state apparatus. Especially security agencies." These were then quotations from Dmitri Orlov, a Russian scientist, engineer and futurologist living in the United States. To conclude with another quote from the classic, I would also quote Martin Heidegger, the greatest thinker of the 20th century, whom I have quoted in my foreign policy speeches here on the remote. He said: "We are bound by technology, whether we adore it or rebel against it. But we will surrender ourselves to this dominance in the most helpless way if we treat technology as something neutral. This approach completely conceals the nature of technology from us. " Thank you!
